   For completeness, the errors in this sign calculation must be relative to two four-arm
probabilities. Let π4 = π4δ (c1 s, c2 ), for fixed suitable constants. Bulk component searches give

                                                E M 4 1{M ≥3} ≤ os (1)π42 ,                 (17.9)
                                                              

                       P(M = 2, an endpoint support is hit) ≤ Cεα π42 ,                    (17.10)

for some α > 0, with the mesh taken sufficiently small at each fixed s, ε. Here is the
exploration underlying these estimates. Around either box the 2M radial separators, cropped
with end margins, give 2M alternating bank arms and at least M distinct actual primal
crossing components of a cropped annulus. Conversely, two distinct actual primal crossing
components force two intervening dual separators, hence four alternating arms in a slightly
narrower annulus. Thus the first two successful discoveries already cost Cπ4 . Expose each
complete actual component, including all its exiting edges revealed closed, and do not count
wire identifications as open paths. The remaining graph is dominated by the original annular
lattice with maximal identifications at its original rims. In separated dyadic rings, a buffered
dual circuit blocks every actual radial crossing with uniformly positive conditional probability.
Each further discovered crossing therefore costs at most Csα . Iterating this bound gives a
summable tail even after multiplication by M 4 . The two box annuli have disjoint bulk collars,
so their first-four-arm costs multiply up to a constant; this proves (17.9). An endpoint hit
additionally requires a bank arm from radius εs to a fixed small multiple of s, in a buffered
region inside the hole of its corresponding four-arm annulus. Both four-arm costs are retained.
The same conditional blocking estimate proves (17.10). In particular an absolute o(1) bound
would not suffice in either estimate.
    Finally there is a compatible positive event of probability at least cπ42 , with c independent
of sufficiently small ε. Prescribe primal east/west and dual north/south landings at each
box annulus, with buffered extensions. Attach the two primal inner landings to small actual
circuits around the positive and negative endpoint disks, respectively, at radii ηs, where the
small constant η > 0 is fixed before choosing ε ≪ η. At the large scale join the inward primal
arms directly between the boxes, and the outward pair around their exterior. Join the upper
dual arms to each other and the lower dual arms to each other between these routes. At
each inner rim join its two dual arms in a central corridor between the terminals. Together
these dual connections form a closed oval separating the two primal groups. Retain one
inner-to-outer landing pairing of comparable probability at the first annulus and its mirror at
the second; the gluing works for either pairing. One of the two pairing classes has at least
half the separated-arm probability, so no additional theorem concerning a prescribed pairing
is needed.
    All these joins can be made in finitely many disjoint tubes of the requested colors, outside
the core annuli except for their landing extensions. Use transverse crossings in overlap

                                               105
rectangles to join the extensions to the tubes. Condition on the complement of one color’s
tubes and apply FKG and buffered RSW there, then do the other color. Since only that color’s
extensions enter its tubes, the requirements are increasing in the appropriate conditional
model. The two core annulus probabilities multiply by the bulk collar comparison. The
resulting positive and negative terminal circuits belong to two distinct actual primal clusters,
the first connecting the two positive terminals and the second the two negative ones.
    There are at least two distinct closed interfaces separating these two connected primal
groups: color parity makes their number even, and at least one is necessary. Each such
interface separates both endpoint pairs and misses their support disks. On the part of this
event with M = 2, the two-loop coefficient is 1/4. Subtracting (17.9) and (17.10), first choose
ε > 0 small and fixed, and then s small. Positivity on all the remaining M = 2 configurations
and (17.8) show
                                   ∥A(E)∥2 = EK ≥ c′ π42 .
Combining this with (17.7) proves lim supδ↓0 π4 ≤ Cs2 . Quasi-multiplicativity and fixed-factor
rim changes give (17.5). Bulk collars give its domain version.

Corollary 17.6 (Macroscopic contacts). In any tight subsequential limit of actual loops
or Dobrushin strands, the interior set of genuinely distinct visits by macroscopic curves
has Hausdorff dimension at most zero, almost surely. Constant parametrization pauses are
discarded. No assertion about boundary contacts is made here.

Proof. Fix an interior compact set and an outer displacement cutoff R > 0. A point with
two genuinely distinct visits, each having displacement at least R, gives four medial passages
across every sufficiently small surrounding annulus. With rim margins, their actual banks give
four alternating arms. A grid cover at scale r has O(r−2 ) cells, each with limiting probability
at most C(r/R)2 . Its expected number of required cells is therefore bounded uniformly in r,
after the mesh limit. For every η > 0 the expected η-dimensional covering content is O(rη );
along dyadic scales it tends to zero almost surely. Countable compact sets, displacement
cutoffs, and positive η prove the claim.


18     The field in regular domains
We now derive the finite-domain statement required for the loop identification. It is not part
of Proposition 17.2.

18.1    Actual walls and elementary bounds
Write C ∈ {P, D} for a color and C ′ for the other. An actual homogeneous wall is a simple
polygon of C-diagonals, whose switches are forced open in color C. A mixed wall consists of
an actual primal-diagonal arc, an actual dual-diagonal arc, and two tile sides joining their
ends. Each tile side is a one-port transition gate. All interior switches not on a wall are
sampled, including a switch whose diagonal endpoints happen both to lie on the wall.

Lemma 18.1 (Vacuum realization of a wall). Conditioning the plane vacuum on exactly
the stated wall switches gives the FK law inside with the indicated actual connections. For
a closed C-wall, the C ′ model on the strictly internal sites and its internal edges has free
boundary condition. In a mixed wall, fixing the binary index at one transition port fixes
the index of the distinguished strand; this condition has probability 1/2 conditional on every
switch configuration.




                                              106
Proof. An exterior connection of either color can enter only through the same-color wall arc,
already connected by actual open edges. Hence it creates no additional interior partition. For
a closed wall no C ′ vertex is wired through that wall, giving the stated free model (equivalently,
apply planar duality). There are exactly two transition ports in the mixed experiment. The
loop through one must leave the interior through the other; its one independent fair index is
fixed by the condition at the first port.

   Set the height to zero on a homogeneous wall. In a mixed wall, take height zero on its
primal arc and choose the distinguished index so that its dual arc has height one. Conditional
on the geometry,
                           hδ (z) = u∗,δ (z) +                                          (18.1)
                                                X
                                                      ϵL 1{z∈int L} ,
                                                   L internal
where the signs are independent and fair. In the homogeneous case u∗,δ = 0; in the mixed
case it is the 0/1 side function of the distinguished strand. Values on the drawn curves are
immaterial in integrals. For f ∈ Cc∞ in the interior, the pairing ⟨hδ , −∆f ⟩ depends only on
switches and binary indices near the support of f : integration by parts writes it as oriented
flux against a compactly supported smooth one-form. It consequently does not depend on
the relative height constant.
Lemma 18.2 (Free-bank estimates). Consider a patch in which the bank color C ′ has no
forced open edge and no boundary identification on any domain wall meeting the patch. The
graph may omit sites or edges. Counts of disjoint medial passages between buffered annular
rims have moments of every order, uniformly in the mesh and in the remaining exterior data.
The probability of an actual C ′ arm across a ratio R/r is at most C0 (r/R)β for some β > 0.
These statements hold as well for a product of free C ′ models on disjoint subgraphs. In a bulk
patch either color may be used.
Proof. Order the medial radial separators and crop their actual C ′ bank paths with margins
at both ends. Different strips cannot communicate across a separator, and each strip is
charged at most twice. Thus many passages force a proportional number of distinct actual
crossing components of the cropped graph. Reveal these components successively from one
rim, ignoring partition jumps. A deleted component does not wire surviving interior sites
through its former neighbors. Since the wall is free for this color, the remaining graph is
dominated by the full annular lattice with maximal ties only at its original rims. A buffered
circuit of the other color blocks actual crossing, with conditional probability bounded below
by bulk RSW. Iteration gives a geometric tail for the component count and hence all its
polynomial moments. Applying these blockers in separated dyadic rings proves the power
bound for one arm. Missing edges or sites only lower actual connectivity; the same comparison
applies to a product on disjoint subgraphs. Enlarge inner radii by a fixed mesh multiple
whenever needed.

     Take now a fixed simple rectilinear polygon V , with two distinct marked transition points
on interiors of straight sides in the mixed case. Approximate it by the corresponding actual
diagonal walls within O(δ), in marked boundary order. Such approximations exist: round
each color arc on its own lattice and adjust its ends by O(δ) to join the two colors by a single
tile side at each transition. This class of regular domains will suffice for the later comparison
argument.
Lemma 18.3 (Moment kernels and compactness). For the regular polygons just specified,
the height moment kernels at distinct interior points z1 , . . . , zm satisfy
                  m
                                                           m
                        hδ (zi ) ≤ Cm 1 + | log(d∗ ∨ δ)|            d∗ = 1 ∧ min |zi − zj |,   (18.2)
                  Y
              E                                                 ,
                                                                              i<j
                  i=1


                                                   107
with d∗ = 1 when m = 1. Here Cm depends on V and m, uniformly over the interior tuples,
including those approaching the boundary. Geometric majorants for the conditional signed
expansions have higher polynomial moments with corresponding logarithmic bounds. Integrated
moments are uniformly integrable; the height laws are tight as local distributions, and their
moment kernels are asymptotically equicontinuous on compact sets of distinct interior tuples.
The count bounds hold also for arbitrarily shaped homogeneous walls in a fixed bounded box
and for products of disks of one wall color.
Proof. A loop winding around z and of diameter in [r, 2r] lies within O(r) of z and supplies a
macroscopic passage in one of a bounded number of annuli of radius comparable with r. For
a homogeneous wall use its free bank color. In a fixed mixed polygon, choose a subpassage
away from the transition points by a fixed fraction of r. The two wall arcs separate linearly
away from a transition, so the chosen patch avoids one of them and has a free bank color.
Lemma 18.2 gives bounded moments per octave. At mesh scale a deterministic local bound
suffices. The same free bank argument works for arbitrary homogeneous wall shapes and for
the stated products.
    Expand (18.1) and average the signs. A loop which survives must be assigned to at
least two of the distinct points and therefore has diameter at least d∗ . The remaining offset
factors have absolute value at most one. Minkowski’s inequality over O(1 + | log(d∗ ∨ δ)|)
octaves gives (18.2) and the asserted higher moment majorants. Integrating the logarithmic
singularities gives uniform integrability. Uniform second moments for compactly supported
Fourier tests give tightness in a sufficiently weak local negative Sobolev space, hence tightness
as distributions.
    To move one point a small distance while the tuple stays distinct, any changed contributing
loop indicator must belong to a loop of diameter bounded below which approaches that point.
In the mixed case a changed offset requires the distinguished strand to approach it. The
bulk one-arm estimate makes these events uniformly rare; the higher count moments control
their contribution. This proves equicontinuity, allowing the O(δ) ambiguity of the drawing.
The same annular passage counts give tight macroscopic curve counts and tightness modulo
reparametrization by the displacement-arc subdivision argument of Section 15.

18.2    Neutral tests through a conditioned wall
Theorem 18.4 (The regular-domain field). Let V be a fixed polygon and let its actual wall
approximations be as above. Then

                         hδ =⇒ u + σΦV          as distributions in V,                    (18.3)

where u = 0 for a homogeneous wall and u is the harmonic extension of the boundary values
0, 1 for a mixed wall. All joint integrated moments converge. At every tuple of distinct fixed
interior positions, the moment kernels converge to those of the same field.
    The proof has three parts. First we transfer local Laplacian tests through separated pieces
of wall. Next we close the gaps between those pieces. Finally we determine the harmonic
ambiguity left by the Laplacian tests.
Lemma 18.5 (Analytic displacement across a vacant slab). Consider two groups of vacuum
insertions supported in finitely many pairwise separated rectangles, with a vacant projection
slab between the two groups. Its normal is parallel to a lattice axis or a diagonal. Normalize
each wall constraint by the probability of its isolated switch constraints; allow bounded charge
and index factors. Translate one whole group farther across the slab. Every subsequential
continuum correlation is real analytic in the translation distance on a neighborhood of [0, ∞).
This assertion also holds with other, previously fixed translations.

                                              108
Proof. First note the needed uniform norm bound. For a wall factor let Wj denote its switch
constraints only, and let pj be their isolated vacuum probability. Allowed lattice translations
and reflections preserve pj exactly. The arbitrary-event comparison across vacant bulk collars
gives
                                             Y 1W j
                                       c≤E          ≤ C.                                 (18.4)
                                              j
                                                 pj

The constants may depend on the fixed gaps and rectangles. A single marked-port projection
contributes exactly 1/2 to the expectation of the positive wall constraints. Bounded charge or
index factors can be omitted in absolute value. The same upper bound applies to separated
subfamilies, their lattice translates, and their reflected copies. In particular the reflected
norms needed below are bounded uniformly in the mesh. No lower bound on an individual,
possibly very rare, wall probability pj is asserted.
   On the algebraic reflected space let Sδ translate an insertion deeper into the half-plane by
an admissible lattice period in the normal direction. Reflection and translation invariance
make this operator symmetric. Cauchy–Schwarz gives

                                      ∥Sδn v∥2 ≤ ∥v∥ ∥Sδ2n v∥.

At fixed mesh every finite insertion is bounded, including its normalizing constants, so the
norms on the right are bounded as the displacement varies. Iterating this inequality proves
∥Sδn v∥ ≤ ∥v∥. It also shows that null vectors remain null. Thus Sδ extends to a self-adjoint
contraction on the reflected Hilbert space. Use Tδ = Sδ2 to obtain a positive self-adjoint
contraction. Write pδ = O(δ) for its physical translation step.
    Reserve a small fixed part of the vacant slab. After absorbing the rest into the positions
of the two base vectors, the correlations have the spectral interpolation
                                D                  E
                                        N +z/pδ
                       Fδ (z) = aδ , Tδ δ         bδ ,        Nδ pδ −→ a > 0.           (18.5)

The vector norms are bounded by (18.4). Spectral calculus therefore makes these a bounded
holomorphic family on every closed half-plane strictly inside Re z > −a. A bounded integer
rounding of the exponent changes a correlation by o(1) locally there: for n of order δ −1 and
bounded real θ,
                                sup |tn+θ − tn | = O(n−1 ).
                                    0≤t≤1

Thus rounding a physical shift by a bounded number of admissible normal translation periods
gives the same limit. Take a diagonal subsequence on a countable array of rational shifts
and apply normal-family compactness to (18.5). Every resulting limit is holomorphic on a
neighborhood of the entire nonnegative real ray, including zero. The assertion for one cut
only uses the other translations as fixed parameters.

Lemma 18.6 (Removal of a local neutral factor). Let finitely many cells of one finite
rectangular grid, separated by positive-width grid gaps, contain normalized wall factors,
bounded index factors, and charge factors. Let one cell contain only

                      Xδ (f ) = exp i⟨hδ , −∆f ⟩ ,            f ∈ Cc∞ (cell; R).
                                                    


In the expectation of the product, this factor may be replaced with error o(1) by
                                                                     !
                                              σ2
                                                         Z
                                µ(f ) = exp −                    2
                                                             |∇f |       .              (18.6)
                                              2

The geometry and all gaps are fixed before δ ↓ 0.

                                                  109
Proof. Put Yδ = Xδ (f ) − µ(f ). If an allowed reflection line separates this cell from all
the others with a strict projection margin, reflection Cauchy–Schwarz proves the claim
immediately. Indeed the squared norm of Yδ tends to zero: the two reflected Laplacian tests
have disjoint supports and their whole-plane Gaussian covariance is
                                          Z
                                     σ2       ∇f · ∇(f ◦ θ) = 0.

Proposition 17.2 consequently factors their characteristic functions. Reflection conjugations
and orientation signs do not change this conclusion. The other reflected vector has bounded
norm by (18.4).
    For general cell placements choose the original cells from a finite rectangular grid with
positive gaps, and call the row and column of Yδ its central row and column. The following
finite displacement procedure creates the required separating line.
   1. Translate all rows above the central row upward, and all rows below it downward, each
      as a single group.
   2. Translate the columns to the right of the central column rightward, and those to the
      left leftward, by amounts larger than the preceding vertical spread.
   3. The central cell is now isolated in its x + y projection. Partition all other cells into
      the groups with larger and smaller x + y projections. Move these groups far in the
      directions (1, 1) and (−1, −1), respectively, until the former are above and to the right
      of all remaining cells and the latter below and to their left.
   4. Move the upper group farther right and the lower group farther downward. All other
      cells then have larger x − y projection than the central cell.
Each movement begins with a vacant projection slab and increases its width. Individual
rectangular collars stay vacant: each translated group moves rigidly, and the two groups
at a cut only become farther apart. Each named directional movement is a separate one-
parameter translation. Empty groups need no movement. The amounts are chosen successively
sufficiently large, with each threshold allowed to depend on all earlier choices.
    In the final configuration a diagonal reflection line separates Yδ , so every limiting correlation
is zero. Apply Lemma 18.5 to the last displacement. Analytic uniqueness extends the zero
correlation from all sufficiently large values to zero. Continue in reverse order. At each
stage the earlier parameters are still sufficiently large for the relevant vacant slab, and the
later ones have been returned to zero. A countable set of rational choices suffices for a
common subsequence. The triangular dependence of the thresholds therefore causes no loss of
parameters. Eventually the correlation at the original placements is zero. Every subsequence
has the same conclusion, proving the o(1) assertion.

    We apply this lemma by cutting small positive-width gaps in the wall. Fix a tuple of
interior positions and group its coincident positions. Choose a finite horizontal and vertical
grid so that every group lies alone in an interior cell with strict margins. The grid lines
miss all corners and transitions, and meet the relevant wall sides transversely. Remove the
wall constraints in a thin strip at each such line. The remaining wall pieces have separated
rectangular supports. The one marked-port projection, if present, belongs to one of those
wall cells. In each interior cell combine all tests assigned to its group into one exponential.
Formula (18.4) bounds the normalizing expectation away from zero and infinity for this fixed
gapped geometry. Successively applying Lemma 18.6 identifies the joint Laplacian-test law
under this gapped conditioning.

Lemma 18.7 (Closing homogeneous wall gaps). For fixed interior charge supports and, when
present, a marked port, the local normalized charge laws with gapped and complete walls

                                                  110
become arbitrarily close as follows: first take the mesh limit at fixed positive gap widths, and
then let those widths tend to zero.

Proof. Consider one gap of width η at a homogeneous wall point. The switches still to be
fixed lie in a ball of radius O(η), and are to be open in the wall color C. First omit the
marked-index condition. In the opposite color C ′ , the gapped model is the larger of the two
models. Couple by exploring all actual C ′ components incident to the fixing edges in this
larger sample, starting from both endpoints and revealing also all exiting edges. Use the
ordered FK conditional probabilities for common queried edges. Explore actual open edges
only, without following boundary partition identifications.
    The only differing fixed data lie on the fixing edges. On the event that the discovered
components stay inside a fixed small homogeneous neighborhood, their outside edges are
closed in both samples. This screens the changes, so the unqueried graphs have identical
induced laws and may be sampled identically. This is also a direct whole-plane exploration
with only specified edge states; it requires no boundary wire jump. The probability that the
exploration leaves the neighborhood is at most C(η/R)β by Lemma 18.2, where R > 0 is its
fixed radius.
    Every discrepant switch is open for C ′ in the larger sample, so both its C ′ vertices belong
to the discovered components. A loop or strand touching a discrepancy in either model has
its actual C ′ bank in those components. On the screening event it therefore cannot reach any
charge support or the marked port, all outside the neighborhood. All remote loops and their
binary indices can be matched exactly. Restoring the marked-index condition changes the
exceptional probability by at most a factor two, since its conditional probability is exactly 1/2.
Repeat at the finitely many gaps and let their widths tend to zero after the mesh limit.

18.3    Identification of the harmonic part
Proof of Theorem 18.4. Take a subsequential distributional limit h, using Lemma 18.3. The
preceding gap construction, Lemmas 18.6 and 18.7 show that, in a neighborhood of every
tuple of interior positions, the joint Laplacian tests have the Gaussian law with covariance
                                        Z
                    E L(f )L(g) = σ 2                       L(f ) = ⟨h, −∆f ⟩.             (18.7)
                                
                                             ∇f · ∇g,
                                         V

For coincident positions all the tests are combined in their single vacant cell before applying
the characteristic-function argument. This detail includes the diagonals of V m for every m.
    More explicitly, the integrated moment bounds give moment distributions of every order
for h, and hence for its Laplacian. On product neighborhoods of every position tuple they
coincide with the Gaussian moment distributions just computed. Tensor test functions,
partitions of unity, and their density in smooth compactly supported tests show equality
on all of V m . This excludes any unseen distribution supported on a coincidence diagonal.
Every linear combination of finitely many L(f ) has the Gaussian moments; Gaussian moment
determinacy identifies its law. Thus L is a centered Gaussian isonormal field on Cc∞ (V ) with
(18.7).
    Extend L by mean-square density to H01 (V ). For a smooth compactly supported density
g, let vg ∈ H01 (V ) solve −∆vg = g weakly, and set

                                         h0 (g) = L(vg ).

Then h0 has the law of σΦV . For f ∈ Cc∞ (V ), v−∆f = f , so ⟨h − h0 , −∆f ⟩ = 0. Consequently

                                        H = h − h0 − u                                     (18.8)


                                               111
is distributionally harmonic, and therefore represented almost surely by a smooth harmonic
function. No independence between H and h0 has been assumed or is needed.
    It remains to show that H = 0. We use moment kernels, not pointwise values of the
limiting random distribution. Let x, y approach distinct boundary points which are neither
corners nor transitions. The uncentered second kernel of h − u tends to zero. To see this
before passing to the limit, use (18.1). In the mixed case the wrong 0/1 side at a point
near its prescribed wall requires the marked strand to cross a short segment from that point
to the wall. It must then leave a fixed local patch, since both marked ports are elsewhere.
Likewise, a loop containing both x and y has diameter bounded below; a segment from x to
the nearby wall must cross it, so the loop approaches that wall point while leaving the patch.
The free bank one-arm estimate makes both events rare. The uniform higher count moments
in Lemma 18.3 absorb their contributions. Finally the deterministic harmonic function u
tends to the prescribed boundary value. The homogeneous case has no marked term and is
the same argument. Thus

                  Kh−u (x, y) −→ 0      at distinct ordinary boundary points.              (18.9)

Kernel convergence and equicontinuity on interior compact sets justify taking the mesh limit
before this boundary limit.
   Let ψ : D → V be conformal. Polygon corner behavior implies that ψ and its inverse
extend Hölder continuously to the boundary. In particular, for some a, C > 0,

                                 |ψ(z) − ψ(w)| ≥ C −1 |z − w|a .

If r, s ≥ 1/2, then |reiθ −seiφ | ≥ c|eiθ −eiφ |. The logarithmic second-kernel bound consequently
gives an integrable majorant
                                                               2
                                    C 1 + log |eiθ − eiφ |                                (18.10)
uniformly over radii approaching one. The same type of bound holds for the Dirichlet Green
kernel, whose value tends to zero at two distinct boundary points.
    Choose nonnegative smooth functions ρt of integral one, each supported on a compact
radial interval in (0, 1) whose two endpoints tend to one. For every integer n, consider the
smooth distribution test which, for an ordinary function v, is

                                            1

                       Bn,t (v) =   ρt (r)               v(ψ(reiθ ))e−inθ dθ dr.

For distributions this is the pullback test; its density in V includes the conformal change-of-

                                   Bn,t (h − u) −→ 0         in L2 .

The Dirichlet kernel gives the same conclusion for Bn,t (h0 ). Hence Bn,t (H) → 0 in L2 .
   The harmonic function H ◦ ψ has Fourier expansion with n-th circle coefficient cn r|n| .

All coefficients, including c0 , vanish almost surely. Thus H = 0, and (18.3) follows for every

                                                 112
19     The endpoint in rough domains
We work at q = 4 throughout this section. We use the whole-plane height convergence of
Proposition 17.2 and its regular-domain form, Theorem 18.4. Write

                                2                                1
                                                  r
                                                      π
                             σ = ,
                              2
                                           λ0 =         ,   σλ0 = .
                                π                     8          2

The normalization of the Dirichlet Gaussian free field is GD = (−∆D )−1 , so its Green function
has singularity (2π)−1 log(1/|z − w|). A signed height has an independent fair unit jump
across each closed contour. Its offset is specified separately. Our purpose is to identify the
conditional field in domains cut out by the contours themselves. Their boundaries need not
be Jordan curves at this stage.

19.1    Pockets and their conditional laws
A wall of color C means an actual connected open wall of that color, rather than an
identification of separated vertices. Denote the other color by C ′ . All the following discrete
drawings may be separated within a distance O(δ), where δ is the mesh, so that their contours
are simple and have the prescribed traversal order.

Lemma 19.1 (Exterior-readable pockets). In a homogeneous C-wall disk, each outer contour
is the external perimeter of an outermost actual C ′ -cluster. Behind it lie pockets bounded by
the maximal simple C ′ -open cycles, ordered by inclusion of their interiors. Their interiors
are disjoint; several pockets can belong to one outer contour. The contours, these cycles,
their assignment to contours, and all switches outside the pocket interiors can be determined
without querying strictly interior switches. Conditional on these data, the pocket interiors
have independent homogeneous C ′ -wall laws.
    In a two-arc disk, first reveal the distinguished path. On either side, its bank together with
that side’s wall arc determines analogous maximal-cycle pockets of the wall color. Conditional
on the path and the exterior pocket data, all these pockets have independent homogeneous
laws. A point wound by a containing contour or side closure, and farther than O(δ) from its
bank walk, lies in one of the corresponding pockets. Every further contour winding that point
is contained in that pocket.

Proof. Draw each cluster as a small regular neighborhood of its actual graph. Its boundary
follows the paired medial pieces within O(δ). Every perimeter behind its external perimeter
lies in a bounded face of that graph. Such a face is enclosed by a simple cycle. Equivalently,
the bank of the external perimeter is a closed graph walk; decomposing this walk into simple
cycles shows that every wound point with the specified clearance belongs to a maximal cycle
interior.
    Two maximal cycle interiors cannot overlap without one containing the other. Otherwise
their boundaries meet at two or more vertices, their union is contained in one biconnected
block, and the simple outer boundary of that block encloses both. This contradicts maximality.
Shared vertices or edges cause no difficulty, since the open interiors remain disjoint.
    We justify the assertion about revealing information. Fix a possible list of cycles and
expose its wall edges and everything outside its open disks. Any connection between vertices
of one cycle that uses its interior already has a connection along that cycle. More strongly,
adding arbitrary interior edges cannot join two distinct exterior biconnected blocks. After
removal of an exterior cut vertex, the attachments belonging to any one wall cycle remain
connected along the rest of that cycle. Thus an interior addition cannot repair an exterior
cut vertex. The exterior block containing a maximal cycle already has all its vertices in


                                               113
that cycle’s closed disk: otherwise the simple external boundary of the block would be a
larger cycle. A new cycle that both enters and leaves a listed disk would have two distinct
wall attachments and would consequently belong to its wall block. It cannot enlarge that
block outside the disk. The same argument applies if several listed disks are traversed, since
they would then have to belong to the same exterior block. Nor can internal changes alter
an external cluster perimeter. Consequently the event specifying the proposed list and its
exterior is measurable from the revealed edges. Each wall is actually connected, so the domain
Markov property gives the asserted product of homogeneous laws.
    For the marked path, close each side using its wall arc and the appropriate half-sides of
the terminal gates. The same-color bank and wall form a closed walk of actual open edges.
The path lies outside every cycle made from this walk, so its exploration queries no switch
strictly inside such a cycle. The preceding argument therefore applies on both sides. Finally,
a further contour leaving a pocket and winding a point inside it would have to surround its
wall cycle. This is impossible because that cycle belongs to an actual cluster connected to
the original wall.

   For a family D of disjoint homogeneous disks, let HD be the signed height with zero offset
and independent laws in the disks. Its value at a point refers to the disk containing that
point.

Lemma 19.2 (Comparison of zero-offset moments). Let D− and D+ be two families of
homogeneous discrete disks of the same wall color. Suppose that each disk of D− is contained
in a disk of D+ . Assume all disks lie in a fixed bounded region. For distinct points z1 , . . . , zm
in the smaller interiors,
                                m                     m
                                      HD− (zj ) ≤ E         HD+ (zj ) + o(1).                 (19.1)
                                Y                     Y
                            E
                                j=1                   j=1

The error tends to zero with the mesh, uniformly when the points have fixed positive pairwise
separation. The statement compares moments, not signed heights in an ordered coupling.

Proof. On graphs of the opposite color, the smaller laws are independent free random-cluster
laws. Their product is stochastically dominated by the restriction of the larger laws: reveal
the remaining edges and use the induced boundary partitions, which dominate free boundary
conditions. Couple these opposite-color configurations by inclusion.
    Consider a smaller outer contour that is relevant to the test points. Its cluster is included
in a larger-model cluster, but the latter may itself be surrounded by a different cluster. Select
the outermost larger contour containing the filled hull of the smaller cluster. One must make
this selection even when the two clusters do not merge. Using nested regular neighborhoods
of included graphs, or the cycle decomposition in Lemma 19.1, shows that this selected
contour contains all the wound test points with clearance. Every relevant smaller maximal
cycle lies in a maximal cycle behind the selected larger contour. In the surrounding case, the
smaller cycle lies in a bounded face of the outer cluster and is therefore enclosed by one of its
maximal cycles.
    Retain only the two sets of exterior and pocket data from this coupling. Now resample
their interiors using the conditional laws of Lemma 19.1. Their wall color has reversed, so
the same free opposite-color comparison applies at the next stage. Iterate. This is a sequence
of couplings of conditional marginals; it does not claim monotonicity of nesting depth in the
original edgewise coupling. At every fixed number of stages it gives inclusion at the same
numerical nesting levels. A smaller contour’s group of test points can merge with another
such group, but cannot split. There is no extra contour of that level between a selected outer
contour and a pocket behind it: the pocket wall is part of that very contour’s cluster.

                                                 114
    To translate this construction into moments, expand each height as the sum of signs of
its surrounding contours and average the signs. A term survives exactly when every assigned
contour receives an even number of the indices 1, . . . , m. Encode that term by the vector of
nesting levels (ℓ1 , . . . , ℓm ) assigned at the respective points. The same vector is admissible in
the larger model: mergers add even multiplicities and hence preserve their parity. Distinct
vectors remain distinct assignments. This is an injection between the terms counted by the
two sign-averaged moments.
    For a surviving term, each assigned contour winds at least two distinct test points and
has diameter bounded below by their separation. All its ancestors have that lower bound as
well. Truncate the number of such contours and ancestors. The free-bank arm bound makes
the event that any of these contours passes within O(δ) of a test point negligible. On its
complement all the required pocket containments hold. Lemma 18.2 and the higher count
moments in Lemma 18.3 remove the exceptional event and then the truncation by uniform
integrability. This proves (19.1).

19.2    Geometry before identification
The arm and passage estimates give three properties of every subsequential contour limit
that we shall use. We spell out their endpoint content to avoid importing a crossing estimate
at a true q = 4 boundary.

Lemma 19.3 (Status disks and boundary traversal). For every positive-diameter limiting
contour, and for the limiting distinguished path, the set of distinct multiple visits in the
original-domain interior has Hausdorff dimension at most zero. Deterministic interior points
are almost surely avoided. At every interior trace point there are disks of each winding status
arbitrarily nearby. In the marked case the statuses are read after closing the path on either
wall arc. No nonconstant subtraversal of a limit lies entirely on the original Jordan boundary.
    These statements and curve tightness hold both for the regular wall experiments and for
the marked Jordan approximations in the theorem.

Proof. The first two assertions follow from Proposition 17.5 and Corollary 17.6, together
with the one-arm estimate. For the two-status assertion use the attached-circuit argument
of Section 15, with both banks retained. Here is the only modification required at q = 4.
Enlarge the first crossing band a little laterally, and give a label only to an actual component
containing a crossing of the original core. After a component has been searched and removed,
comparison with full wiring on the enlarged band’s boundary is still valid. A buffered
opposite-color transversal extending past both lateral ends of the core blocks any further
core crossing. Bulk RSW gives this blocker a uniformly positive probability. The number
of labels is therefore tight, and each label represents an actual connection in the revealed
past. The leftmost crossings from a label’s bottom sources in successive core slabs can now
be used exactly as in the attached-circuit argument; the fresh-side circuits lie in interior balls.
For a distinguished path, close it along the remote wall when reading the two statuses. The
argument also works with exposed bulk data outside the test patch. Only buffered bulk RSW
is used [10].
    For the boundary assertion, fix a deterministic boundary point away from the marks. A
macroscopic passage approaching it supplies an actual arm of the bank color that is not wired
there. In local annuli this arm is bounded by the free-bank comparison. Along a primal wired
arc the dual bank uses internal square faces and has no dual exit through that arc. Along
the complementary arc the primal bank uses actual sampled edges and has no primal ties.
Exterior dual identifications occur only beside the complementary arc. Turns can be followed
by adjacent actual bank edges within O(δ). Any other induced ties in a buffered annulus


                                                115
away from the other arc attach the surviving graph only at its rims. Thus the same arm
bound applies in the specified Jordan approximations.
    A nonconstant boundary subtraversal would cover an arc and hit a point of a fixed
countable dense subset away from the marks, by a macroscopic passage. The preceding bound
excludes this. For tightness, at each displacement scale use a finite cover of subpassage tests
away from the two marks. Every displacement contains a smaller displacement a fixed fraction
of that scale away from both marks. Marked uniform Jordan convergence and separation of
the two boundary arcs allow its annulus to be cropped in a neighborhood missing at least
one arc. The same passage estimates apply there. Interior no-spur and multiple-visit tests
always retain positive clearance from the original boundary.

19.3    Protected cuts and pointed kernels
We next give the deterministic approximation needed for the conditional field. A single limit
contour may have several open winding components; filling in its hull would incorrectly join
some of them.

Lemma 19.4 (Outer comparisons with protected cuts). Let Ln be simple discrete contour
closures in a bounded Jordan domain V , converging uniformly modulo reparametrization
to L. The closures may include one original wall arc. Assume the geometric properties of
Lemma 19.3. Let T be the trace of L, and let U and E be, respectively, the open sets of
winding status one and zero off T , with infinity in E. Then the components of U are simply
connected pointed kernels of the interiors of Ln .
    For every β > 0 there exist simple regular comparison polygons Bj , and protecting balls
centered on T ∩ V or ∂V , such that:
   (i) the pointed kernels of Bj at points of U are precisely the separate components of U ;
  (ii) every compact subset of U is eventually contained in Bj ;
 (iii) at each fixed j, deleting the part of a sufficiently fine discrete pocket outside Bj changes
       edges only in finitely many protecting balls, of radii ri , with i riβ −→ 0 as j → ∞;
                                                                          P

 (iv) Bj is connected to a region outside V .
The finite protecting selection is made after constructing the cuts.

Proof. Every point of T ∩ V is approached by E, by the two-status property. Points of T ∩ ∂V
are approached by the exterior of V . Hence

                                           E = C \ U.                                       (19.2)

Compact subsets of U persist in the approximating interiors by winding stability. Conversely,
every excluded point is arbitrarily close to persistent exterior points, by (19.2). Thus the
component containing a fixed point of U is exactly its pointed kernel. Kernels of simply
connected domains are simply connected.
   Let M ⊂ T ∩ V be the distinct multiple-visit set, after constant pauses have been erased.
Given η > 0, cover all of M by a countable collection of open balls Di , centered on M , with

                                 rad(Di )β < η,         sup rad(Di ) < η.
                            X

                             i                           i

This is possible because dimH M ≤ 0. Set G = E ∪ i Di .
                                                             S

   We first show that any x ∈ E ∩ V can be joined to the exterior through G, after adding
at most one arbitrarily small ball centered on ∂V . If the closure of the path component of x
in G reaches ∂V , such a ball connects it to the Jordan exterior. Otherwise that component


                                                  116
has a compact frontier S ⊂ V \ G separating x from infinity. The set S meets T only at
unique-preimage points; its other points lie in U .
     There are polygonal closed walks, arbitrarily close to S, of nonzero winding about x.
To see this, grid the bounded component of C \ S containing x, retain its edge-adjacent
squares reachable from x without meeting S, and trace their external perimeter. We can
lift these walks, with an error tending uniformly to zero, into the closed disks bounded
by Ln . Near a point of S ∩ U , a fixed interior ball persists. Near a point z ∈ S ∩ T , all
boundary parameters approaching z tend to its unique preimage under L. Nearby points
of the approximating closed disk can therefore be connected near z: first go by a closest
segment to the boundary, and then use the short boundary arc between the resulting nearby
parameters. The closest segments stay in the closed disk. The errors are uniform along the
walk; otherwise compactness would give one of the two local situations just considered and
contradict failure of uniformity. The lifted closed walk still winds about x, whereas x lies
outside the approximating disk. This contradicts contractibility of that disk and proves the
escape assertion.
     Choose a large square Q containing V in its interior. Take finite increasingly fine nets of
E ∩ Q whose vertices belong to E. Join each net vertex to an exterior collar of Q using the
escape assertion. The finitely many additional boundary-centered balls can have arbitrarily
small total β-content. Select a finite polygonal tree joining these paths to one root on ∂Q,
otherwise lying in the open allowed region; gridding the paths and deleting cycles gives such
a tree with axis-parallel edges. Remove a sufficiently thin polygonal neighborhood of this
tree, attached to ∂Q. Using narrow rectangular links and square junctions leaves one simple
rectilinear polygon B. Keep an open part of the collar outside V in B.
     As the nets become finer, the removed tree approaches every point of E ∩ Q. Any part of
it entering U lies in the protecting balls, whose radii tend to zero and whose centers lie on T
or ∂V . Hence compact subsets of U persist, while the pointed kernels cannot extend through
an excluded point. This proves the first two assertions.
     For the finite selection, fix the tree and its closed thin neighborhood. It is compact and
lies in the union of E and the countable protecting balls. A finite subcover uses compact
subsets of E and finitely many of those balls, with a positive margin. The compact subsets
of E are disjoint from the closed disks bounded by Ln for all large n. Thus only the finitely
many selected balls can contain cuts affecting the discrete pockets. Their total content is no
larger than that of the full cover. This is why the countable cover is used before selecting the
tree. A further O(δ) adjustment makes the comparison polygon a regular wall polygon on
the appropriate color lattice, without losing the protecting margins.

19.4    Transfer of the conditional field
Proposition 19.5 (Rough-interior moment transfer). Condition on exterior pocket data
converging to a containing contour as in Lemma 19.4. At every fixed distinct tuple of points
in U , the joint moments of the residual zero-offset height converge to the Wick moments of
independent fields σΦ0Ω in the components Ω of U . This holds along every such convergent
sequence of exterior data. Several containing contours at one generation may be treated
simultaneously, taking the product over them.

Proof. Write β > 0 for the power in the free-bank arm bound. Fix a comparison polygon B
and the finite protecting balls supplied by Lemma 19.4. In every pocket delete opposite-color
edges outside the lattice approximation of B. Equivalently, intersect its set of elementary
wall-color cells with the corresponding cell set of B, retaining only opposite-color edges
between surviving adjacent cell centers. All changes are in the protecting balls.



                                              117
    The original opposite-color laws are product free laws. Couple them with the modified
laws by exploring, in the larger law, the actual clusters incident to the deleted edges. Once
those clusters are exposed, all discrepancies are shielded and the unqueried graphs have the
same conditional law. The argument is the same as the gap-closing coupling in Section 18.
If the ball radii are ri and e > 0 is fixed, the probability that a discrepancy reaches farther
than e from their centers is at most

                                                 (ri /e)β .                                (19.3)
                                             X
                                         C
                                             i

Indeed every such discrepancy supplies an actual opposite-color arm in the original product
free law, dominated by the full-lattice comparison. Contours through changed switches have
banks in these discovered clusters and obey the same locality bound. Choose e smaller
than the tuple’s separation and its clearance from T ∪ ∂V . Every sign-averaged residual
moment term uses a contour of at least the pair-separation diameter. The macroscopic count
moments and Hölder’s inequality therefore make the moment error tend to zero with the
total protecting content in (19.3).
    We verify the laws and offsets of the retained regions. Each side-connected component of
the retained wall-color cells is again a simple polygon disk. Every globally missing cell has a
path of missing cells to the exterior: it is outside at least one of the two simple grid polygons
being intersected. Thus a retained component has no hole. Its boundary cannot pinch at a
vertex with two opposite retained cells in that component and two opposite missing cells. The
retained pair would be connected by a side-adjacent path, while the missing pair is connected
through the exterior; these two disjoint connections between alternating sectors contradict
planar separation. Apply this argument separately to each old pocket. The retained free
opposite-color graph hence factors into independent homogeneous disk laws.
    Each retained component also reaches an old pocket wall. Start from one of its cells
and take a side-adjacent path in B to the part of B outside V . The first exit from the
old pocket crosses an old wall edge; the path before that exit remains in the same retained
component. The component therefore has an actual wall-color connection to the old wall. No
exterior contour can surround it and add an unrecorded height jump. Its residual offset is
zero. Finally, a connected compact neighborhood of any test point began in one old pocket
for large n: the bank walk tracking Ln winds around that compact, so Lemma 19.1 places it
in a pocket.
    For the upper bound, apply Lemma 19.2 from the retained disks into B, and then apply
Theorem 18.4. For the lower bound, make no deletions. Choose one regular polygon disk
compactly inside each involved component Ω, containing its test points, and compare their
product laws into the original pockets. Such inner disks can exhaust each simply connected
Ω in the pointed-kernel sense.
    Dirichlet Green functions converge along simply connected pointed kernels. Between
points whose limiting kernels are different, they tend to zero; this follows by conformal
mapping convergence, or by the vanishing probability of crossing the separating bottleneck
before exit. Therefore the Wick kernels of both the inner polygons and the protected outer
polygons converge to those of independent Dirichlet fields in the components Ω. Take the mesh
limit first at fixed polygons and protectors; next improve the inner and outer approximations
and let their total protecting content vanish. The two bounds coincide. Already assigned
ancestor signs, or the marked path’s side constants, multiply lower-order residual moments
and are restored afterwards. They do not enter the moment comparison.




                                                 118
20     Identification at the endpoint and the boundary convention
20.1    A global conditional decomposition
Take a joint subsequential limit of the height and the distinguished path in a regular two-arc
polygon. For a homogeneous initial square, take a joint limit of the height and the full contour
collection, including nesting levels, parent relations, and independent fair signs. These marked
geometric limits can be obtained at countably many positive-diameter cutoffs decreasing to
zero, moving a cutoff when necessary to avoid atoms. At each cutoff there are only a tight
number of relevant contours and ancestors. Thus finite marked relations stabilize, and a
diagonal extraction retains every positive limiting contour. The signs remain conditionally
independent and fair given the geometry, which was sampled without using them.
    Fix a nesting level k in the square. Every deterministic interior point lies almost surely
in a positive winding region at that level. Indeed alternating actual circuits on successively
separated bulk scales force arbitrarily many surrounding contours. The domain Markov
bounds and bulk RSW make the failure probability tend to zero as the number of scales
grows, with the mesh limit taken first at each finite selection of scales. The same statement
holds for Lebesgue-almost every point by Fubini. Winding regions of different contours at a
fixed level are disjoint, as their discrete predecessors are. Positive coincident traversal arcs
would also contradict the multiple-visit dimension bound. In the marked case the two side
closures likewise cover almost every point away from their traces.
    Let Fk denote the limiting exterior geometry and signs through level k, or let F denote
the limiting marked path in the two-arc experiment. Let O be the collection of components
of the corresponding open winding regions. Define v on each component to be the sum of its
containing signs through level k, or its marked side value 0 or 1. Its value on the remaining
area-zero set is immaterial.

Proposition 20.1 (Conditional field in all complementary components). Conditional on the
stated limiting exterior data, the height has the distributional decomposition
                                           law
                                        h = v+σ                 Φ0Ω ,                            (20.1)
                                                       X

                                                       Ω∈O

where the Dirichlet fields are independent and extended by zero. The identity holds as an
identity of distributions on the whole initial-domain interior, not merely after restriction to
individual components.

Proof. For a test function f , the independent sum on the right exists in L2 , since Green-
function monotonicity gives
        X ZZ                                          ZZ
                    |f (z)|GΩ (z, w)|f (w)| dz dw ≤             |f (z)|GV (z, w)|f (w)| dz dw.   (20.2)
       Ω∈O     Ω2                                          V2


The same estimate, with a fixed negative Sobolev norm, defines the sum as a random
distribution.
    Use a representation on which the extracted geometric data converge. A fixed distinct
tuple of deterministic interior points is almost surely eventually covered, with positive limiting
clearance, by the relevant winding components. Its offsets converge. Conditional on the
discrete exterior pocket data, including the actual cycle walls, its residual moments converge
by Proposition 19.5. That proposition applies along every realized convergent data sequence;
it requires no regularity of the unexplored cycle walls beyond the proved pocket properties.
    To pass to conditioning only on the stated limiting data, multiply the discrete moment
identities by a bounded continuous function of those data. The necessary uniform integrability

                                                 119
follows from Lemma 18.3. At level k, terms already assigned to ancestors have offsets bounded
by k. The remaining sign-averaged terms require shared contours of at least the relevant
pair-separation diameter, and their counts have all polynomial moments under the initial law.
The marked offset is bounded by one. Thus, for each m, a bound of the form
                                     m
                   Cm,k 1 + | log d∗ |        d∗ = min 1, |zi − zj | : i ̸= j ,

and its higher-moment versions apply also before coverage by the limiting components has
stabilized. Such bounds are integrable on tuples in compact subsets of V , including near
diagonals.
    Smear the identities against tensor products of smooth test densities. Fubini and uniform
integrability pass every joint moment to the limit, with every bounded continuous geometric
multiplier retained. The almost-everywhere coverage removes the remaining set from these
integrals. Higher even moments give uniform integrability of the smeared variables themselves.
A countable determining family of test densities then yields the conditional moment identities
for distributions. For each fixed conditional geometry, the Gaussian law with the bounded
offset on the right of (20.1) is moment determining; equivalently, apply the one-dimensional
Gaussian moment criterion to every rational linear combination of finitely many tests. Hence
the moment identities identify the conditional law. Because the tests range over the entire
initial domain, this argument also excludes a residual distribution supported on the area-zero
complement of the components.

20.2    Local-set uniqueness with the exact height normalization
We use two continuum identifications. For the zero-boundary GFF, the two-valued bounded-
type thin local set with values −a, b is unique and field measurable when a, b > 0 and
a + b ≥ 2λ0 . At a = b = 2λ0 it is the non-nested CLE4 gasket, with independent fair labels
on its holes [2, Propositions 1 and 2 and Sections 4 and 6.3].
    For boundary data we use Theorem 3.21 of Aru et al. [3], with its immediately preceding
boundary criterion. In the specialization needed here, the initial domain is a disk, u is
bounded harmonic with finitely piecewise constant boundary data in [−a, b], and a + b ≥ 2λ0 .
Uniqueness holds for bounded-type thin local sets whose total conditional harmonic function
is constantly −a or b on each connected boundary component of each complementary domain.
The interval here is closed: boundary values equal to −a or b are permitted. The standard
completed chordal SLE4 level line, for boundary data −λ0 , λ0 on the two arcs, has exactly
this conditional field decomposition [28].

Lemma 20.2 (The limiting sets are bounded-type thin local sets). For the first contour level
in the square, let
                                   A=V \        Ω.
                                            [

                                                 Ω∈O

In the marked experiment let A be the path trace together with ∂V . After the normalizations
below, these are bounded-type thin local sets. In the homogeneous case their harmonic values
are ±2λ0 . In the marked case their total harmonic values are ±λ0 .

Proof. In the homogeneous case, write Φ = h/σ and m = v/σ. Theorem 18.4 says that Φ
is a zero-boundary GFF. At the first level v ∈ {−1, 1}, so m ∈ {−2λ0 , 2λ0 }. In the marked
case put
                             h − 1/2                    v − 1/2
                        X=            = Φ + u,     m=           − u,
                                 σ                         σ
where Φ is a zero-boundary GFF and u is the bounded harmonic function with boundary data
−λ0 , λ0 on the respective arcs. Here |m| ≤ 2λ0 and m + u ∈ {−λ0 , λ0 } on each component.

                                              120
    In either case Proposition 20.1 gives the full conditional Dirichlet law of Φ − m in
V \ A. This law depends on the exterior data only through A. Conditioning down to (A, m)
proves locality. Moreover m is an ordinary bounded function, integrated over V \ A, and
the distributional identity has no additional part supported on A. This is thinness. The
uniqueness of a local set’s harmonic distribution from the field and set is also part of the
local-set formalism [3, Lemma 2.3(1)]; no extra choice of component labels is being added at
this step.
    For completeness, A is connected and contains the entire Jordan boundary. Its comple-
mentary components are simply connected by Lemma 19.4. If A were disconnected, split
it into two nonempty disjoint compact sets, putting the connected ∂V in the first. A fine
polygonal boundary around part of the second, at positive distance from the first and from
∂V , would be a closed walk in a component of V \ A with nonzero winding about a point of
A. This contradicts that component’s simple connectivity. Thus A is a nontrivial connected
compact set. It has no isolated points, and no component disjoint from ∂V . These facts
verify the topological condition in the definition of a bounded-type thin local set. Conformal
transport to a disk preserves all these assertions.

Proposition 20.3 (Regular marked endpoint). The distinguished curves in the regular
two-arc experiments converge as ordered curves, up to increasing changes of parametrization,
to chordal SLE4 between the prescribed marks.

Proof. Apply the boundary-data uniqueness theorem with a = b = λ0 . The initial domain,
after conformal transport, has one complementary component, which is not a singleton. The
boundary function has two changes and takes values in the closed allowed interval. Its interior
range meets (−λ0 , λ0 ) by the strong maximum principle. The height-gap condition holds
with equality. In every component of V \ A, the total conditional mean is constant −λ0 or λ0 ,
so the stated boundary criterion holds on every boundary component. Finally Lemma 20.2
supplies thinness, boundedness, and the required topology. All hypotheses of Aru et al. [3,
Theorem 3.21] are therefore met.
    The completed Schramm–Sheffield level line coupled to the same field satisfies these same
conditions. Uniqueness identifies the limiting closed set, together with its harmonic labels,
with that level line plus the original boundary. In particular its interior trace is the simple
chordal SLE4 arc, which has no hits on the interiors of the two boundary arcs.
    This closed-set statement identifies the original ordered curve as well. Any extra boundary
point off the identified compact arc has a neighborhood disjoint from that arc; reaching it would
force a nonconstant boundary subtraversal, excluded by Lemma 19.3. On a simple arc, any
genuine backtracking repeats a nondegenerate subarc. Such a subarc has positive Hausdorff
dimension, contradicting the dimension-zero multiple-visit bound. After suppressing constant
pauses, the curve therefore traverses the arc once, in the prescribed endpoint order.

20.3    Contours, labels, and all nesting levels
Proposition 20.4 (Endpoint contours in a square). In a homogeneous square, the entire
signed contour hierarchy converges to nested CLE4 , with independent fair signs at each
generation. The identification includes each individual contour as a simple-loop element, its
parent, and its traversal order up to the loop convention.

Proof. The first-level set A in Lemma 20.2 has harmonic values ±2λ0 . Ordinary two-valued-set
uniqueness identifies its coupling with the field as the labelled non-nested CLE4 gasket.
   We must still identify the discrete contour elements, since one such element could a priori
have several winding components. Every positive contour has an interior-status disk, by the
two-status property and the exclusion of boundary-only traversal. Thus it winds at least

                                              121
one hole of the identified gasket. Select two distinct holes by the first points they contain
in a fixed countable dense set. Conditional on the gasket A, the two unit offsets in our
construction satisfy

                E[v(Ω1 )v(Ω2 ) | A] = P{Ω1 and Ω2 belong to one contour | A}.               (20.3)

Indeed the signs were independent and fair given the entire unlabelled contour geometry;
different contours contribute zero covariance, whereas the same contour contributes one. In
the unique two-valued-set coupling, different CLE4 holes have independent fair labels, so the
left side of (20.3) is zero. Countability excludes all grouping of distinct holes.
    Consequently each positive contour winds exactly one simple CLE hole. Its full interior
trace equals that hole’s Jordan boundary: the two-status property puts every trace point on
the boundary of its winding region, and winding stability puts that boundary in the trace.
Additional boundary pieces are excluded as in Proposition 20.3. The limiting winding is
one. Any extra covering or backtracking repeats a nondegenerate subarc and violates the
dimension-zero bound. Thus the original contour is exactly the corresponding simple-loop
element, not just a set with the same filled interior.
    Proceed conditionally through the hierarchy. Given the geometry and labels through
an identified level, the residual fields in its holes are independent zero-boundary GFFs by
Proposition 20.1. The next-level decomposition, with the parent constant subtracted, makes
its missing set in each hole a thin local set with harmonic values ±2λ0 . Child winding
components lie in the parent by discrete winding stability at points with clearance. They
are simply connected and cover almost every point, by the arguments already given. Every
positive child has an interior-status disk and is supported on the closure of its winding regions
by the two-status property. Thus the same topology and thinness verification applies inside
each parent hole.
    Conditional uniqueness, including measurability from the respective residual field, gives
independent fresh CLEs in the parent holes. Equation (20.3) and the trace argument again
identify every individual child and its sign. Its parent can be read at any interior disk point off
the traces. Induction identifies all finite generations. Finally every positive-diameter contour
and all its ancestors occur in the retained finite-cutoff hierarchies, whose counts are tight.
Sending the cutoff to zero identifies the full collection in the stated matching topology.

Corollary 20.5 (Endpoint whole-plane ensemble). The infinite-volume free critical FK
loop ensemble at q = 4 converges to the nested whole-plane CLE4 ensemble, in the spherical
matching metric for all loops.
Proof. Use the expanding-square coupling of Section 15. Its endpoint ingredients are bulk
estimates: monotone boundary coupling and exploration of actual boundary connections
couple the infinite-volume free switches with the square switches deep inside a large square,
except when a boundary connection reaches the inner region. Actual dual circuits on successive
annuli block that event, uniformly even under the maximal boundary law. Actual-color
annular blockers likewise make a loop from a fixed disk reaching the coupling boundary
unlikely. The finite-square limit is given by Proposition 20.4.
    Nested simple CLEs in domains of conformal radius tending to infinity converge locally to
nested whole-plane CLE; their spherical whole-plane law is Möbius invariant [16, Theorem 1,
Proposition 4, and Section 3.2]. Its range 8/3 < κ ≤ 4 includes the present endpoint, and
centered expanding squares meet its conformal-radius condition.
    To pass from the local comparison to the spherical metric, first fix a spherical diameter
cutoff. Loops lying entirely outside a sufficiently large fixed disk are below that cutoff. With
high probability, every other loop above the cutoff remains inside the switch-coupling region,
by the preceding escape bound. The number of such loops is tight, and the finite-square

                                               122
matching identifies them bijectively with their traversal order. Take the mesh limit, then
enlarge the square and the fixed disk, and finally send the diameter cutoff to zero. This is
the required global matching statement, including all nesting levels.

20.4    The prescribed marked Jordan approximations
It remains to transfer Proposition 20.3 to the precise wired/free edge convention of the
theorem. This transfer uses actual color walls, so that a boundary identification is never
mistaken for a path in the lattice.

Proposition 20.6 (Endpoint marked Jordan convergence). For every marked uniform Jordan
approximation specified in the theorem, its critical q = 4 distinguished curve converges to
chordal SLE4 in the limiting marked domain.

Proof. Use the two-change disk drawing from Lemma 11.1: place tiles on every polygon-graph
edge, give boundary edges distinct exterior tabs, and put local caps along the wired primal
and complementary dual arcs. Flattening the tabs and separating the drawing in a thin collar
changes the ordered curve by only O(δn ) in uniform distance. Boundary edges on the wired
primal arc may be fixed open, while edges on the free arc retain their sampled law.
    The primal bank of the distinguished path, closed along the primal wall, is a walk of
actual open edges in the wired cluster. On the other side, the actual internal dual bank, its
exits to exterior copies across the free arc, and the local complementary-wire links give the
dual closing walk. All artificial dual links and copies stay within O(δn ) of that free arc. No
dual exit through the primal wired arc is used. At fixed bulk points away from limiting traces
these walks therefore have stable complementary winding statuses 1 and 0. Lemma 19.3
gives tightness, the two-status property, and the restrictions on multiple visits and boundary
traversal for these drawings.
    Construct a regular comparison polygon V + close to the limiting marked domain V with
the following two strict separations: the original primal wired arc, including its endpoints,
lies outside V + ; and the comparison dual wall lies outside V . All margins are fixed before
taking the mesh limit. One construction uses plane Schoenflies coordinates for ∂V . Move
the primal portion slightly inward, extending it a little beyond both marks, then rise outside
the unit circle near the two ends and put both comparison transitions there. Keep the
whole comparison dual portion outside the unit circle. Approximate the resulting simple
curve by a simple rectilinear polygon, preserving all strict margins, and place the transitions
inside straight sides. Its actual primal-open and dual-open wall approximations are regular
experiments. Reverse the colors to construct V − : the original complementary arc is outside
V − , and the comparison primal wall is outside V . Both comparisons approach the same
marked Jordan domain as the offsets vanish.
    Couple the original law with that in V + so that every original primal open edge in the
common region is open in the comparison. To check the boundary partition needed for this
ordering, restrict the original configuration to the common edges. Any connection departing
from the comparison disk can induce ties only at its actual primal wall vertices: its dual wall
and transitions lie outside the entire original closure, while the original wired vertices lie
outside the comparison. The comparison connects all those primal wall vertices by fixed open
wall edges. Its induced partition therefore dominates the original one, and random-cluster
monotonicity gives the coupling. The cut is along a primal lattice wall, so no unaligned edge
crossing is being treated as a wall vertex.
    Every piece inside V + of the original primal closing bank walk belongs to the comparison
primal wall cluster: follow its original connection to the outer wire and take its first exit from
V + . Suppose a test point has original primal winding status but comparison dual status,


                                               123
with positive clearance. On the comparison dual side it can be joined to the comparison dual
wall without crossing the comparison primal wall cluster, and hence without crossing the
original closing walk inside V + . From that wall, which is outside the entire original polygon,
it can escape to infinity through the original exterior. This contradicts the original winding.
Thus original primal status implies comparison primal status.
    For V − apply exactly the dual ordering. Original dual identifications and artificial bank
links lie beside the complementary arc, outside V − . In the common region the dual walk uses
only internal dual steps, and departing connections induce ties only at the aligned comparison
dual wall vertices. An originally wired primal edge admits no dual crossing step. Original
dual status therefore implies comparison dual status. The free-bank arm bound removes
near-hits and tile ambiguities at any prescribed finite tuple of bulk points.
    Let In (z) be the original primal-side indicator, and let In− (z), In+ (z) denote those in the
two comparisons. The separate monotone couplings give, for every fixed finite tuple z, the
stochastic bounds
                           (In− (z))z∈z ⪯ (In (z))z∈z ⪯ (In+ (z))z∈z ,                      (20.4)
up to errors vanishing with the mesh. At fixed comparison polygons the limiting laws are
the regular chordal SLE4 laws by Proposition 20.3. Now let the offsets vanish. Conformal
maps and their boundary extensions converge under marked uniform Jordan convergence,
using uniform local connectivity of these boundary parametrizations. The two comparison
SLE laws therefore have the same limit. Deterministic interior points avoid SLE, so their
side-status arrays converge as well. Equation (20.4) identifies every finite-dimensional status
law of an original subsequential limit.
    On a fixed countable dense set, the statuses determine the interior trace: an interior trace
point has both statuses in every neighborhood, whereas a point off the trace has a small disk
of constant status. This follows from Lemma 19.3. Thus the limiting interior trace is the
SLE trace. No boundary traversal and the dimension-zero multiple-visit bound identify the
complete ordered curve exactly as in Proposition 20.3. Every tight subsequence has the same
law, proving the full-sequence assertion.
